### 本测试块考点概述 (Block Overview):
本测试块涵盖横纹肌全维对比（骨骼肌 vs 心肌）、平滑肌机电偶联生化级联与自主神经血管药理学：横纹肌形态分歧（骨骼肌多核无分支周边核 vs 心肌单/双核中央核具分支与闰盘）；膜结节分歧（骨骼肌 A-I 带三联体 vs 心肌 Z 线二联体且 T 管管径粗 5 倍）；钙触发机制分歧（骨骼肌 DHPR-RyR1 纯机械拔栓偶联独立于胞外钙 vs 心肌 Cav1.2 钙内流触发 RyR2 发生 CICR 绝对依赖胞外钙）；强直收缩分歧（骨骼肌不应期极短易于强直 vs 心肌有效不应期长达 250 ms 覆盖收缩期绝不强直以保障心室舒张充盈）；变力性调节分歧（骨骼肌纯躯体神经冲动分级 vs 心肌交感 Beta-1-PKA 级联磷酸化 Cav1.2、RyR2、受磷蛋白产生正性肌力与正性舒张）；受磷蛋白（Phospholamban：抑制 SERCA2a，PKA 磷酸化解除抑制加速心肌舒张，快骨骼肌缺乏）；长度-张力工作点分歧（骨骼肌在静息时位于最佳 L0 顶点 vs 心肌位于上升支低段，静脉回心增加拉长心肌产生 Frank-Starling 机制）；平滑肌超微结构（无横纹、无肌小节、无 Z 盘、完全缺乏肌钙蛋白复合体）；细肌丝锚定（胞质密体 dense bodies 与膜密斑含 alpha-actinin，连接结蛋白 desmin 与波形蛋白 vimentin 中间丝框架）；收缩丝几何优势（高肌动蛋白/肌球蛋白比 12-15:1 伴侧向极性粗肌丝无裸区，允许大幅滑行缩短 > 70% 腔内容积）；钙-钙调蛋白级联（胞质钙升高结合钙调蛋白 Calmodulin 形成 Ca2+-CaM 复合体，变构激活肌球蛋白轻链激酶 MLCK）；MLCK 催化（特异性磷酸化肌球蛋白 20 kDa 调节轻链 MLC20 的 Ser-19 位点，解除头部自抑制激活横桥与肌动蛋白结合及 ATP 水解）；肌球蛋白轻链磷酸酶（MLCP：由 PP1c 催化亚基与 MYPT1 靶向亚基构成，水解脱去 Ser-19 磷酸使平滑肌舒张）；闸门状态（Latch-bridge state：已结合横桥在未脱离时被 MLCP 脱磷酸化形成脱离极慢的锁定横桥，在极低 ATP 消耗下终生维持高血管张力与括约肌阻力）；钙调磷酸结合蛋白（Caldesmon 与 Calponin：结合肌动蛋白并立体抑制 ATPase，钙结合或磷酸化可解除抑制）；多单位平滑肌（少缝隙连接、神经独立支配、无自发节律，睫状肌、虹膜、输精管）vs 单单位平滑肌（密布缝隙连接合胞体传导、自发慢波起搏、胃肠道、子宫、膀胱、微小动脉）；平滑肌钙通道体系（电压门控 Cav1.2、受体门控 ROCC、库操纵 SOCC / Orai1-STIM1、牵张激活阳离子通道 TRPC6）；药物机械偶联（Pharmacomechanical coupling：激动剂偶联 Gq 产生 IP3 诱发肌浆网释钙，电位完全无偏移即可强烈收缩）；Gq 血管收缩轴（去甲肾上腺素 alpha-1、血管紧张素 II AT1、内皮素-1 ETA 经 Gq-PLC-IP3-DAG-PKC 升钙收缩）；钙敏感化机制（RhoA/Rho 激酶 ROCK 轴：受体激活 RhoA 活化 ROCK，特异性磷酸化并抑制 MLCP 的 MYPT1 亚基，使 MLC20 维持磷酸化，在低钙下维持持续强力收缩）；钙脱敏（PKG 活化 MLCP 或 PKA 磷酸化抑制 MLCK，使平滑肌在钙不降时发生舒张）；Beta-2 受体舒张级联（沙丁胺醇、特布他林激活 Gs-cAMP-PKA，PKA 磷酸化抑制 MLCK、开放大电导钙激活钾通道 BK_Ca 超极化膜、加速钙回泵引发支气管扩张与子宫保胎平滑肌松弛）；一氧化氮（NO）- cGMP - PKG 血管舒张轴（内皮 eNOS 催化 L-精氨酸生成 NO 气体，旁分泌入血管平滑肌激活可溶性鸟苷酸环化酶 sGC 升高 cGMP 激活 PKG，激活 MLCP 并关闭 Cav1.2 促成强力扩血管）；硝酸酯类药理（硝酸甘油需线粒体醛脱氢酶 ALDH2 酶解脱氮释放 NO，选择性扩静脉容量血管降低前负荷；硝普钠非酶自分解释放 NO 伴氰化物毒性，均衡扩动静脉降低前后负荷）；PDE-5 抑制剂（西地那非、他达拉非抑制 cGMP 降解，与硝酸酯同服引发致死性暴发性低血压为绝对禁忌）；sGC 刺激剂（利奥西呱双重直接刺激与增敏 sGC 治疗肺动脉高压与 CTEPH）；内皮素受体拮抗剂（波生坦、安贝生坦阻断 ETA/ETB 治疗 PAH，强制监测月度肝功与严禁妊娠）；前列环素途径（依前列醇、曲前列尼尔、司来帕格激活 IP 受体经 Gs-cAMP 强力扩张肺血管）；钙通道阻滞剂（双氢吡啶类氨氯地平/硝苯地平高选择性扩张阻力小动脉，毛细血管前压升高致踝部凹陷性水肿与反射性心动过速；非双氢吡啶地尔硫䓬/维拉帕米均衡心脏结区阻滞）；尼莫地平（高脂溶性穿透 BBB 预防蛛网膜下腔出血破裂动脉瘤后迟发性脑血管痉挛）；钾通道开放剂（米诺地尔开放 KATP 超极化膜强力扩阻力动脉，强烈反射性交感与水钠潴留必须合用 Beta 阻滞剂与利尿剂，多毛症）；胃肠道平滑肌与 Cajal 间质细胞（ICC 产生慢波基础电节律 BER：胃 3 次/分、十二指肠 12 次/分、回肠 8-9 次/分，慢波去极化超阈值激发钙动作电位尖峰触发机械蠕动）；膀胱平滑肌神经支配（逼尿肌 M3 受体收缩排出 vs Beta-3 受体舒张储尿；内括约肌 Alpha-1 收缩控尿；外括约肌阴部神经骨骼肌随意控制）；膀胱过度活动症（M3 拮抗剂奥昔布宁具中枢抗胆碱谵妄认知损害风险 vs 纯 Beta-3 激动剂米拉贝隆通过 Gs-cAMP 舒张逼尿肌无中枢抗胆碱副作用）；气道平滑肌三重调节（M3 迷走收缩抗胆碱异丙托溴铵/噻托溴铵；Beta-2 舒张沙丁胺醇/沙美特罗；半胱氨酰白三烯 CysLT1 强力收缩拮抗剂孟鲁司特）；子宫平滑肌（催产素受体与前列腺素 F2alpha/E2 经 Gq 促宫缩，雌激素上调 Cx43 缝隙连接实现临产合胞体同步强力收缩，硝苯地平与特布他林保胎）；肌源性反应（Bayliss 效应：跨壁压增高牵张 TRPC6 阳离子通道去极化激活 Cav1.2 发生自主代偿性收缩维持恒定血流，为脑肾血流自身调节基石）；代谢性自身调节（局部腺苷、K+、H+、CO2 与低氧超越交感收缩引发功能性交感溶解与活动性充血）；雷诺现象（指端小动脉 Alpha-2C 反应亢进与冷诱发痉挛，白-蓝-红三相改变，二氢吡啶 CCB 治疗）；低氧性肺血管收缩（HPV：肺血管在肺泡低氧下独特收缩以优化 V/Q 匹配，全肺弥漫低氧致肺动脉高压与肺心病）；高原肺水肿（HAPE：快速登高致严重不均一 HPV，无保护毛细血管承受静水压破裂应力衰竭，钙阻滞剂与他达拉非解救）；高血压急症小动脉平滑肌坏死（血压 > 180/120 mmHg 冲垮自身调节，纤维素样坏死与葱皮样增生）；动脉粥样硬化平滑肌表型转换（PDGF 诱发收缩型平滑肌转化为合成型平滑肌迁入内膜分泌胶原形成纤维帽）；Mönckeberg 动脉中层硬化（中膜钙化形成不闭塞管腔的坚硬管状血管，ABI 假性偏高 > 1.4 无间歇性跛行）；巨细胞动脉炎（肉芽肿性破坏内弹性板与中膜平滑肌，颞肌下颌跛行与缺血性视神经病变失明，大剂量激素急救）；终极综合病例大考：COPD、冠心病合并雷诺现象患者在吸入麻醉与钙阻滞剂/Beta 阻滞剂作用下骨骼肌、心肌与平滑肌机电偶联全面鉴别诊断剖析。

<a id='question-101'></a>

### Question 101: Striated Muscle Comparison: Cellular Morphology and Syncytial Architecture
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Skeletal muscle fibers are multinucleated, unbranched, and have peripheral nuclei, whereas cardiac muscle cells (cardiomyocytes) are typically uninucleated (or binucleated), branched, and have central nuclei. This difference reflects their distinct developmental origins and functional requirements. (骨骼肌纤维是多核的、未分支的，且核位于外周；而心肌细胞通常是单核（或双核）、分支的，且核位于中央。这反映了它们不同的发育起源和功能需求。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A medical student is reviewing high-power light microscopy images of muscle biopsies in the histology laboratory. Image A shows a large, elongated cell with multiple, elongated nuclei arranged peripherally just beneath the cell membrane. The cell appears unbranched and contains prominent striations. Image B shows shorter, branched cells with one or two rounded nuclei located centrally within each cell. These cells also exhibit striations and appear connected end-to-end. The student is asked to identify the histological feature that reliably distinguishes the muscle type shown in Image A from the muscle type shown in Image B.

(A) Presence of intercalated discs connecting adjacent cells.
(B) Central location of nuclei within the cells.
(C) Branched morphology of the individual cells.
(D) Multinucleated nature of the individual fibers with peripherally located nuclei.
(E) Presence of prominent striations within the cytoplasm.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Image A description:** Large, elongated cell; multiple, elongated nuclei; peripheral nuclei location; unbranched; striations. This describes a skeletal muscle fiber.
- **Image B description:** Shorter, branched cells; one or two rounded nuclei; central nuclei location; striations; connected end-to-end. This describes cardiac muscle cardiomyocytes.
- **Question:** Asks for a histological feature that *reliably distinguishes* skeletal muscle (Image A) from cardiac muscle (Image B) on cross-sectional light microscopy.
- **Option (A):** Intercalated discs are characteristic of cardiac muscle, connecting cardiomyocytes. Skeletal muscle lacks these. This *is* a distinguishing feature, but it's a feature of cardiac muscle, not skeletal muscle. The question asks for a feature distinguishing *between* the two.
- **Option (B):** Central nuclei are characteristic of cardiac muscle. Skeletal muscle has peripheral nuclei. This *is* a distinguishing feature, but it describes cardiac muscle.
- **Option (C):** Branched morphology is characteristic of cardiac muscle. Skeletal muscle is unbranched. This *is* a distinguishing feature, but it describes cardiac muscle.
- **Option (D):** Multinucleated fibers with peripheral nuclei are characteristic of skeletal muscle. Cardiac muscle is typically uninucleated or binucleated with central nuclei. This directly contrasts the morphology of the two muscle types described.
- **Option (E):** Both skeletal and cardiac muscle are striated muscles, meaning they both exhibit prominent striations due to the organized arrangement of actin and myosin filaments within sarcomeres. This is a shared feature, not a distinguishing one.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
Skeletal muscle fibers and cardiac muscle cells (cardiomyocytes) are both types of striated muscle, characterized by the presence of sarcomeres, which give them a striped appearance under the microscope. However, their cellular morphology reflects their distinct developmental origins and functional roles.

- **Skeletal Muscle:** Develops from the fusion of multiple embryonic myoblasts into a large, multinucleated syncytium called a muscle fiber. These fibers are typically long, cylindrical, and unbranched. The numerous nuclei are located peripherally, just beneath the sarcolemma (plasma membrane). This arrangement allows for efficient protein synthesis throughout the large cytoplasmic volume and facilitates rapid transmission of action potentials along the fiber surface. Skeletal muscle is under voluntary control via somatic motor neurons.

- **Cardiac Muscle:** Develops from individual myoblasts that do not fuse. Cardiomyocytes are typically shorter, branched cells, allowing for complex interconnections. Each cell usually contains one or two centrally located, rounded nuclei. The branching and end-to-end connections via specialized junctions called intercalated discs (containing fascia adherens, desmosomes, and gap junctions) facilitate rapid and coordinated contraction of the heart muscle. Cardiac muscle is under involuntary control via the autonomic nervous system and intrinsic pacemaker activity.

The question asks for a feature that distinguishes skeletal muscle from cardiac muscle. Option (D) accurately describes the characteristic morphology of skeletal muscle fibers: multinucleated (due to myoblast fusion) and having nuclei located peripherally. This contrasts directly with the typical uninucleated (or binucleated) and centrally nucleated cardiomyocytes of cardiac muscle. Options (A), (B), and (C) describe features characteristic of cardiac muscle (intercalated discs, central nuclei, branching), not skeletal muscle, or a shared feature (striations, option E). Therefore, the multinucleated nature with peripheral nuclei is the defining histological characteristic of skeletal muscle presented in the vignette that distinguishes it from cardiac muscle.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Presence of intercalated discs connecting adjacent cells. This is a key feature of *cardiac* muscle, facilitating electrical and mechanical coupling between cardiomyocytes. Skeletal muscle lacks intercalated discs. While it distinguishes the two, the option describes a feature of cardiac muscle, not skeletal muscle.
- (B): Central location of nuclei within the cells. This is characteristic of *cardiac* muscle cells (cardiomyocytes). Skeletal muscle fibers have peripheral nuclei. This is a distinguishing feature, but it describes cardiac muscle.
- (C): Branched morphology of the individual cells. This is characteristic of *cardiac* muscle cells (cardiomyocytes), allowing for complex network formation. Skeletal muscle fibers are unbranched. This is a distinguishing feature, but it describes cardiac muscle.
- (E): Presence of prominent striations within the cytoplasm. This is a shared characteristic of *both* skeletal and cardiac muscle, as both are striated muscles containing organized sarcomeres. Therefore, it does not distinguish between the two.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key histological difference between skeletal muscle and cardiac muscle lies in their cellular structure: skeletal muscle fibers are multinucleated with peripheral nuclei and are unbranched, while cardiac muscle cells (cardiomyocytes) are typically uninucleated (or binucleated) with central nuclei and are branched, connected by intercalated discs. Remember these morphological distinctions for USMLE Step 1. Differential: Smooth muscle lacks striations and has a different cellular structure (spindle-shaped, single central nucleus).

### Question 102: Striated Muscle Ultrastructure Comparison
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The ultrastructural organization of the transverse tubule (T-tubule) and sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) differs significantly between skeletal and cardiac muscle, forming distinct functional units (triads vs. dyads) at different locations relative to the sarcomere (A-I junction vs. Z-line). (横向小管和肌浆网在骨骼肌和心肌中的超微结构组织存在显著差异，形成不同的功能单位（三联体 vs. 二联体），且其在肌节相对的位置也不同（A-I 结合处 vs. Z 线）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
Dr. Anya Sharma, a renowned electron microscopist specializing in muscle physiology, is examining high-resolution transmission electron micrographs of both skeletal and cardiac myocytes. She observes that in skeletal muscle fibers, the transverse tubules (T-tubules) are narrow, invaginations of the sarcolemma located precisely at the A-I band junction. Each T-tubule is closely associated with two enlarged terminal cisternae of the sarcoplasmic reticulum, forming a characteristic structure. In contrast, when analyzing cardiac myocytes, she notes that the T-tubules are significantly wider and are positioned at the Z-lines. Each T-tubule is associated with only one terminal cisterna (SR expansion). Dr. Sharma wants to confirm her observations regarding the specific structural junction formed by the T-tubule and SR in the heart. Which of the following accurately describes this junction and its location in cardiac myocytes?

(A) A triad, located at the A-I junction, consisting of one T-tubule flanked by two terminal cisternae.
(B) A triad, located at the Z-line, consisting of one T-tubule flanked by two terminal cisternae.
(C) A diad, located at the A-I junction, consisting of one T-tubule paired with one terminal cisterna.
(D) A diad, located at the Z-line, consisting of one T-tubule paired with one terminal cisterna.
(E) A monad, located at the M-line, consisting of one T-tubule without associated terminal cisternae.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The vignette describes an electron microscopy study comparing skeletal and cardiac muscle ultrastructure.
- Key observation 1: Skeletal muscle T-tubules are narrow, at A-I junctions, and associated with two terminal cisternae (forming a triad).
- Key observation 2: Cardiac muscle T-tubules are wide, at Z-lines, and associated with one terminal cisterna (forming a diad).
- The question asks specifically about the structure and location of the T-tubule/SR junction in *cardiac* myocytes.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Skeletal Muscle Excitation-Contraction Coupling:** The sarcolemma (cell membrane) and the T-tubule membrane are continuous. The T-tubules penetrate deep into the muscle fiber, precisely at the A-I band junction (where the thick filament is only in the A band, and the thin filament extends into the I band). At this junction, the T-tubule is in close proximity to the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR), which stores calcium ions (Ca2+). This close association forms a *triad*, consisting of one central T-tubule flanked laterally by two enlarged terminal cisternae of the SR. During muscle fiber depolarization, the action potential travels down the T-tubules. Voltage-gated dihydropyridine receptors (DHPRs) in the T-tubule membrane act as voltage sensors. These DHPRs are mechanically coupled to ryanodine receptors (RyRs) located on the SR membrane within the terminal cisternae. Depolarization of the T-tubule activates the DHPRs, which then physically open the RyRs, causing a massive release of Ca2+ from the SR into the sarcoplasm. This Ca2+ binds to troponin C, initiating the cross-bridge cycle and muscle contraction. The extensive SR and the triad structure allow for rapid and uniform Ca2+ release throughout the large skeletal muscle fiber.

- **Cardiac Muscle Excitation-Contraction Coupling:** Cardiac myocytes are shorter and branch, requiring a different arrangement. The T-tubules are much wider (up to 5 times) than in skeletal muscle and are located at the Z-lines (the boundary between adjacent sarcomeres). Each T-tubule is associated with only one terminal cisterna (SR expansion), forming a *diad*. The DHPRs (L-type Ca2+ channels) are located in the T-tubule membrane, and the RyRs are located on the adjacent SR membrane. Depolarization of the T-tubule membrane opens the DHPRs, allowing Ca2+ to enter the cell from the extracellular space. This influx of extracellular Ca2+ acts as the primary trigger for RyR activation, leading to Ca2+ release from the SR. The amount of Ca2+ stored in the cardiac SR is less than in skeletal muscle, making cardiac contraction more dependent on extracellular Ca2+ influx. The diad structure, located at the Z-line, facilitates this process.

- **Applying to the Question:** The question specifically asks about cardiac myocytes. The vignette describes the cardiac structure as wide T-tubules at Z-lines associated with one terminal cisterna. This corresponds to a diad located at the Z-line.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes a triad located at the A-I junction. This is characteristic of *skeletal* muscle, not cardiac muscle. Incorrect location and structure.
- (B): Describes a triad located at the Z-line. While the Z-line is the correct location for the cardiac junction, the structure is a diad (one T-tubule, one cisterna), not a triad (one T-tubule, two cisternae). Incorrect structure.
- (C): Describes a diad located at the A-I junction. This is the correct structure (diad) but the incorrect location (A-I junction is skeletal muscle). Incorrect location.
- (E): Describes a monad located at the M-line. A monad (single T-tubule without associated SR) is not a standard term for the functional unit, and the M-line is the center of the sarcomere, not the location of the T-tubule/SR junction in either skeletal or cardiac muscle. Incorrect structure and location.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The key difference in excitation-contraction coupling between skeletal and cardiac muscle lies in the ultrastructure of the T-tubule/SR junction: triads at A-I junctions in skeletal muscle vs. dyads at Z-lines in cardiac muscle. This structural difference reflects functional differences, including reliance on extracellular Ca2+ in cardiac muscle.
- **Differential:** Remember that skeletal muscle relies heavily on SR Ca2+ release triggered by DHPR-RyR coupling within a triad at the A-I junction, while cardiac muscle relies on both SR Ca2+ release (triggered by extracellular Ca2+ influx via DHPRs acting as voltage-gated Ca2+ channels) and direct Ca2+ influx, occurring within a diad at the Z-line.

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Striated Muscle Calcium Release Mechanisms
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The fundamental difference in the trigger for sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) calcium release between skeletal muscle (electromechanical coupling) and cardiac muscle (calcium-induced calcium release, CICR). (骨骼肌的电机械耦合 vs 心肌的钙诱导钙释放)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A team of muscle biophysicists is conducting comparative studies on excitation-contraction coupling (ECC) in isolated single fibers. They are meticulously examining the molecular events initiating calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) in response to membrane depolarization. In a fast-twitch skeletal muscle fiber, they observe that depolarization of the transverse tubule (T-tubule) membrane directly leads to the opening of the ryanodine receptor (RyR1) on the SR, releasing a large amount of calcium into the cytoplasm. This occurs without any significant influx of extracellular calcium through voltage-gated calcium channels in the T-tubule. In contrast, when studying an isolated ventricular myocyte, they find that depolarization during the plateau phase (Phase 2) of the action potential causes a small influx of extracellular calcium through L-type voltage-gated calcium channels (Cav1.2) located in the T-tubule. This influx of calcium into the dyadic cleft is essential for triggering the opening of the cardiac ryanodine receptor (RyR2) on the SR, leading to a much larger release of calcium from the SR stores. The biophysicists want to identify the mandatory initial trigger for SR calcium release specifically in the cardiac ventricular myocyte.

(A) Direct mechanical coupling between the dihydropyridine receptor (DHPR) and the ryanodine receptor (RyR1) upon membrane depolarization.
(B) Influx of extracellular calcium through L-type voltage-gated calcium channels (Cav1.2) binding to and opening the ryanodine receptor (RyR2).
(C) Depolarization of the T-tubule membrane causing a conformational change in the dihydropyridine receptor (DHPR) that directly opens the ryanodine receptor (RyR2).
(D) Binding of intracellular calcium to troponin C, initiating the cross-bridge cycle.
(E) Influx of extracellular sodium through voltage-gated sodium channels, causing membrane depolarization and subsequent SR calcium release.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The vignette compares skeletal muscle and cardiac muscle ECC.
- Skeletal muscle: Depolarization -> T-tubule DHPR (Cav1.1) -> Direct mechanical link -> SR RyR1 opens -> Ca2+ release. No significant extracellular Ca2+ influx needed.
- Cardiac muscle: Depolarization -> T-tubule DHPR (Cav1.2) -> L-type Ca2+ channel opens -> Small Ca2+ influx -> Binds to SR RyR2 -> RyR2 opens -> Large Ca2+ release.
- The question asks for the *mandatory trigger* for SR Ca2+ release in *cardiac ventricular myocytes*.
- The key difference highlighted is the *calcium-induced calcium release (CICR)* mechanism in cardiac muscle, which requires extracellular Ca2+ influx.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Cardiac Muscle ECC (CICR):**
    1.  Action potential propagates down the T-tubule.
    2.  Depolarization opens L-type voltage-gated calcium channels (DHPR/Cav1.2) located in the T-tubule membrane, specifically within the dyadic cleft (the close apposition of the T-tubule and SR).
    3.  A small amount of extracellular Ca2+ enters the myocyte through these L-type channels. This influx is crucial; it's the "trigger calcium".
    4.  This trigger calcium binds to specific binding sites on the cytosolic face of the ryanodine receptor (RyR2) located on the SR membrane within the dyadic cleft.
    5.  Binding of Ca2+ to RyR2 induces a conformational change, opening the RyR2 channel.
    6.  The opened RyR2 channel allows a massive efflux of Ca2+ stored within the SR lumen into the myoplasm (cytoplasm). This is the "release calcium".
    7.  The increased myoplasmic Ca2+ concentration binds to troponin C, initiating the cross-bridge cycle and muscle contraction.
- **Skeletal Muscle ECC (Electromechanical Coupling):**
    1.  Action potential propagates down the T-tubule.
    2.  Depolarization causes a conformational change in the voltage sensor portion of the dihydropyridine receptor (DHPR/Cav1.1) located in the T-tubule membrane.
    3.  This conformational change is directly transmitted via a physical (mechanical) link to the ryanodine receptor (RyR1) on the adjacent SR membrane.
    4.  The mechanical interaction directly opens the RyR1 channel.
    5.  Ca2+ is released from the SR into the myoplasm.
    6.  No significant influx of extracellular Ca2+ is required to trigger SR Ca2+ release in skeletal muscle. The small Ca2+ influx that *does* occur through Cav1.1 is insufficient to trigger RyR1 opening and is primarily involved in other cellular processes like excitation-transcription coupling.
- **Conclusion:** The mandatory trigger for SR calcium release in cardiac ventricular myocytes is the influx of extracellular calcium through L-type calcium channels (Cav1.2), which then binds to and opens the RyR2 receptor (CICR).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Direct mechanical coupling between the DHPR and RyR1 is the mechanism in *skeletal* muscle, not cardiac muscle. Cardiac muscle uses RyR2, and the trigger is calcium influx, not direct mechanical linkage. This option confuses the mechanisms of skeletal and cardiac muscle.
- (C): Direct mechanical coupling between the DHPR (specifically Cav1.2 in cardiac muscle) and RyR2 is incorrect. While DHPR and RyR2 are physically close in the dyadic cleft, the DHPR in cardiac muscle functions as a voltage-gated calcium channel, and its opening leads to Ca2+ influx, which *then* triggers RyR2. There is no direct mechanical link like in skeletal muscle. This option describes the skeletal muscle mechanism applied incorrectly to cardiac muscle.
- (D): Binding of intracellular calcium to troponin C is a *consequence* of SR calcium release, not the *trigger* for it. Troponin C binding initiates the cross-bridge cycle, leading to contraction, but it doesn't cause the SR to release calcium. This option confuses the downstream events of ECC with the initial trigger.
- (E): Influx of extracellular sodium through voltage-gated sodium channels is primarily responsible for the rapid depolarization phase (Phase 0) of the action potential in both skeletal and cardiac muscle. While essential for initiating the action potential, it is not the direct trigger for SR calcium release. The trigger for SR release occurs later during the plateau phase (Phase 2) in cardiac muscle, mediated by L-type calcium channels. This option confuses the role of sodium channels in action potential generation with the specific trigger for SR calcium release.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key difference between skeletal and cardiac muscle excitation-contraction coupling lies in the trigger for sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium release. Skeletal muscle uses electromechanical coupling (direct physical link between DHPR/Cav1.1 and RyR1), while cardiac muscle uses calcium-induced calcium release (CICR), requiring extracellular calcium influx through L-type calcium channels (DHPR/Cav1.2) to trigger RyR2 opening. Remember: Skeletal = Mechanical Link; Cardiac = Calcium Trigger.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Striated Muscle Comparison: Extracellular Calcium Dependence
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Cardiac muscle contraction is absolutely dependent on extracellular calcium influx (via L-type Ca2+ channels) to induce calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) through ryanodine receptors (RyR2), a process known as calcium-induced calcium release (CICR). Skeletal muscle contraction, however, relies primarily on calcium release from the SR triggered by direct mechanical coupling between voltage-gated dihydropyridine receptors (DHPRs) and ryanodine receptors (RyR1), making it largely independent of extracellular calcium influx for initiating contraction. (心脏收缩依赖胞外钙离子内流触发肌浆网钙释放；骨骼肌收缩主要依赖肌浆网钙释放，由二氢吡啶受体与肌浆网受体直接机械耦合触发，对胞外钙离子内流不依赖。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A research laboratory is investigating the fundamental differences in excitation-contraction coupling between isolated skeletal muscle fibers and isolated cardiac myocytes. Two separate experimental setups are prepared. In Setup A, isolated frog sartorius muscle fibers (skeletal muscle) are placed in a physiological salt solution (PSS) containing 5 mM NaCl, 4 mM KCl, 1 mM MgSO4, 10 mM glucose, and 50 mM HEPES buffer, with a normal extracellular calcium concentration of 2 mM. The muscle fibers are stimulated electrically at 100 Hz, producing robust tetanic contractions. In Setup B, isolated adult rat ventricular myocytes (cardiac muscle) are placed in an identical PSS, also with a normal extracellular calcium concentration of 2 mM. These myocytes are paced electrically at 1 Hz, exhibiting normal rhythmic contractions.

The researchers then modify the PSS for both setups. A new solution is prepared containing 5 mM NaCl, 4 mM KCl, 1 mM MgSO4, 10 mM glucose, 50 mM HEPES buffer, 2 mM EGTA (a calcium chelator), and 0 mM CaCl2 (zero extracellular calcium).

Setup A (skeletal muscle) is perfused with the zero-calcium PSS. The muscle fibers continue to generate strong tetanic contractions, although the duration might slightly decrease over time.

Setup B (cardiac myocytes) is perfused with the zero-calcium PSS. Within approximately 15-30 seconds, the cardiac myocytes cease contracting altogether, despite continued electrical pacing. The cells remain electrically excitable (action potentials can still be recorded), but no mechanical contraction occurs.

What is the primary physiological reason for this differential response to the absence of extracellular calcium between skeletal muscle and cardiac muscle?

(A) Skeletal muscle relies on calcium influx through L-type calcium channels (Cav1.2) to depolarize the T-tubule membrane, whereas cardiac muscle relies on sodium influx through voltage-gated sodium channels.
(B) Skeletal muscle stores sufficient calcium within the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) to sustain contraction for hours, while cardiac muscle SR calcium stores are rapidly depleted without extracellular calcium influx.
(C) Cardiac muscle contraction requires extracellular calcium influx through L-type calcium channels (Cav1.2) to trigger calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) via ryanodine receptors (RyR2), whereas skeletal muscle relies on direct mechanical coupling between DHPRs and RyR1s for SR calcium release.
(D) Skeletal muscle utilizes troponin C, which binds calcium with high affinity, allowing contraction even at very low intracellular calcium concentrations, while cardiac muscle troponin C has a lower affinity for calcium.
(E) The absence of extracellular calcium prevents the formation of the excitation-contraction coupling triad in skeletal muscle, but not in cardiac muscle.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Setup A (Skeletal Muscle):** Normal PSS (2 mM Ca2+) -> Robust tetanic contractions. Zero-Ca2+ PSS (EGTA + 0 mM CaCl2) -> Continued strong tetanic contractions. This indicates skeletal muscle contraction does not require extracellular calcium for initiation.
- **Setup B (Cardiac Muscle):** Normal PSS (2 mM Ca2+) -> Normal rhythmic contractions. Zero-Ca2+ PSS (EGTA + 0 mM CaCl2) -> Cessation of contraction within seconds despite continued electrical pacing. This indicates cardiac muscle contraction absolutely requires extracellular calcium.
- **Question:** Why the difference? The question asks for the *primary physiological reason* for the differential dependence on extracellular calcium.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Cardiac Muscle Excitation-Contraction Coupling:** An action potential travels down the T-tubule. Depolarization of the T-tubule membrane opens voltage-gated L-type calcium channels (Cav1.2, also known as DHPRs in cardiac muscle). Influx of extracellular Ca2+ through these channels triggers the opening of nearby ryanodine receptors (RyR2) on the SR membrane. This process is called Calcium-Induced Calcium Release (CICR). The release of a large amount of Ca2+ from the SR into the cytoplasm increases intracellular [Ca2+], which binds to troponin C, initiating cross-bridge cycling and contraction. If extracellular Ca2+ influx is blocked (by EGTA or channel blockers), there is no trigger for RyR2 opening, minimal Ca2+ release from the SR, and thus no contraction.
- **Skeletal Muscle Excitation-Contraction Coupling:** An action potential travels down the T-tubule. Depolarization of the T-tubule membrane causes a conformational change in the voltage-gated dihydropyridine receptors (DHPRs). These DHPRs are physically linked to ryanodine receptors (RyR1) on the SR membrane. This mechanical coupling directly opens the RyR1 channels, causing a massive release of Ca2+ from the SR into the cytoplasm. This process does *not* require extracellular Ca2+ influx. The released Ca2+ binds to troponin C, initiating cross-bridge cycling and contraction. Since the trigger for SR release is the DHPR conformational change (caused by T-tubule depolarization), contraction can continue as long as the SR stores contain sufficient calcium, even in the absence of extracellular calcium.
- **Comparison:** The key difference lies in the trigger for SR calcium release. Cardiac muscle requires extracellular Ca2+ influx (CICR), while skeletal muscle relies on direct mechanical coupling between DHPRs and RyR1s. Therefore, removing extracellular calcium abolishes contraction in cardiac muscle but not in skeletal muscle.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Both skeletal and cardiac muscle rely on depolarization of the T-tubule membrane (primarily via voltage-gated sodium channels, although L-type Ca2+ channels contribute significantly to the plateau phase in cardiac muscle) to initiate excitation-contraction coupling. The difference is not in the initial depolarization mechanism but in how that depolarization triggers calcium release. This option incorrectly states the roles of sodium and calcium channels in T-tubule depolarization as the primary difference.
- (B): While skeletal muscle SR stores are large, the statement that cardiac muscle SR stores are "rapidly depleted" without extracellular calcium influx is misleading. The issue isn't depletion; it's the *lack of initial release* triggered by CICR. Without the initial Ca2+ influx triggering RyR2, there is no significant Ca2+ release from the SR, and thus no contraction. The primary reason for cessation is the lack of trigger, not rapid depletion of stores.
- (D): Both skeletal and cardiac muscle use troponin C to bind calcium and initiate contraction. While there might be subtle differences in calcium affinity, this is not the primary reason for the differential dependence on extracellular calcium. The fundamental difference is in the mechanism of SR calcium release initiation.
- (E): The excitation-contraction coupling triad (T-tubule, SR, DHPR/RyR) is present and functional in both skeletal and cardiac muscle. The difference is not the presence or absence of the triad but the specific mechanism by which the triad components interact to release SR calcium. In cardiac muscle, the triad requires extracellular Ca2+ influx; in skeletal muscle, it does not.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cardiac muscle contraction is absolutely dependent on extracellular calcium influx via L-type Ca2+ channels to trigger CICR, whereas skeletal muscle contraction is largely independent of extracellular calcium influx, relying instead on direct mechanical coupling between DHPRs and RyR1s. This difference explains why calcium channel blockers (like verapamil or diltiazem) have a much greater negative inotropic effect on cardiac muscle than on skeletal muscle. Key differential: Cardiac muscle = CICR dependent; Skeletal muscle = DHPR-RyR1 direct coupling dependent.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Cardiac Muscle Electrophysiology and Tetanus
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The prolonged absolute refractory period (ERP) in cardiac muscle, specifically the duration of Phase 2 (plateau phase) of the action potential, prevents summation of contractions and tetanus, ensuring diastolic filling. (心脏肌细胞动作电位持续时间长，绝对不应期长，防止肌张力收缩叠加和破伤风，确保舒张期充盈。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of palpitations and shortness of breath. His heart rate is 160 bpm, and his blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg. An ECG shows supraventricular tachycardia (SVT). During the SVT episode, the patient's cardiac output is maintained, although his heart feels like it is "racing." A physiology student observing the patient asks why the heart muscle, despite contracting so rapidly, does not enter a state of sustained contraction (tetanus) like skeletal muscle might during intense exercise. The student recalls that cardiac muscle action potentials differ significantly from skeletal muscle action potentials. Which electrophysiological characteristic of the cardiac myocyte action potential is primarily responsible for preventing the heart from undergoing tetanic contractions?

(A) The rapid upstroke (Phase 0) of the cardiac action potential, mediated by fast voltage-gated sodium channels, ensures rapid depolarization and contraction initiation.
(B) The presence of gap junctions between cardiac myocytes allows for rapid spread of the action potential, synchronizing contractions across the myocardium.
(C) The prolonged Phase 2 (plateau phase) of the cardiac action potential, due to the influx of calcium ions through L-type calcium channels and efflux of potassium ions, results in a long effective refractory period (ERP).
(D) The relatively short duration of the cardiac muscle twitch (approximately 250 ms) compared to the refractory period allows for rapid cycling between contraction and relaxation.
(E) The reliance on extracellular calcium influx for excitation-contraction coupling in cardiac muscle, unlike skeletal muscle which primarily uses intracellular calcium release, dictates the duration of the action potential.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient has SVT (rapid heart rate).
- The question asks why the heart *doesn't* tetanize despite the rapid rate.
- This points to a fundamental difference between cardiac and skeletal muscle physiology regarding contraction summation and tetanus.
- The question specifically asks for the *electrophysiological* feature responsible.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Cardiac Action Potential:** The cardiac action potential is characterized by a long duration (250-300 ms) primarily due to the prolonged Phase 2 plateau. This plateau is caused by a balance between the inward movement of Ca2+ through L-type voltage-gated calcium channels (ICa,L) and the outward movement of K+ through various voltage-gated potassium channels (e.g., Ito, IKr, IKs).
- **Effective Refractory Period (ERP):** The ERP is the period during which a cell cannot be re-excited, regardless of the stimulus strength. In cardiac muscle, the ERP lasts almost as long as the entire action potential duration, largely coinciding with the Phase 2 plateau.
- **Cardiac Muscle Twitch:** The mechanical twitch duration in cardiac muscle is approximately 250 ms.
- **Tetanus Prevention:** Tetanus occurs when successive stimuli arrive before the muscle has fully relaxed, leading to summation of contractions and a sustained, maximal force. For tetanus to occur, the ERP must be shorter than the twitch duration, allowing for repeated stimulation during the contraction phase.
- **Cardiac Muscle vs. Skeletal Muscle:**
    - **Skeletal Muscle:** Action potential is very short (~1-2 ms). The absolute refractory period is also very short (~2-3 ms). The twitch duration is ~100-200 ms. Because the ERP is much shorter than the twitch duration, repetitive stimuli can occur during the contraction phase, leading to twitch summation and tetanus.
    - **Cardiac Muscle:** The ERP is nearly as long as the twitch duration (~250 ms). By the time the cardiac myocyte has repolarized and recovered its excitability (end of ERP), the mechanical twitch has already begun to relax. Therefore, the next action potential arrives only after the muscle has started relaxing, preventing summation and tetanus.
- **Why this is crucial:** Preventing tetanus is vital for the heart. If the heart tetanized, the ventricles would remain contracted, preventing diastolic filling. This would lead to a drastic reduction in stroke volume and cardiac output, resulting in circulatory collapse. The heart needs to relax between beats to fill with blood.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The rapid upstroke (Phase 0) is due to fast Na+ channels, similar to skeletal muscle, and is responsible for rapid depolarization. While essential for cardiac function, it does *not* explain the prevention of tetanus. The *duration* of the action potential, not the speed of the upstroke, is key.
- (B): Gap junctions are crucial for the functional syncytium of the heart, allowing rapid and coordinated spread of the action potential. This ensures synchronized contraction but does not prevent tetanus. Tetanus prevention is related to the *refractory period* of individual cells, not the intercellular communication.
- (D): The cardiac muscle twitch duration (~250 ms) is relatively long compared to skeletal muscle. The *relationship* between the twitch duration and the ERP is what prevents tetanus. While the twitch duration is a factor, stating it's relatively short is incorrect, and more importantly, it doesn't directly explain the mechanism as well as the long ERP. The key is that the ERP *lasts nearly as long as* the twitch.
- (E): While extracellular Ca2+ influx is essential for excitation-contraction coupling in cardiac muscle (leading to Ca2+-induced Ca2+ release from the SR), and the plateau phase involves Ca2+ influx, the *primary* reason for the long action potential and subsequent long ERP is the balance between Ca2+ influx and K+ efflux during Phase 2, not solely the reliance on extracellular Ca2+. The long ERP is the direct consequence of the prolonged Phase 2, which is electrophysiological.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The prolonged absolute refractory period in cardiac muscle, dictated by the long Phase 2 plateau of its action potential, prevents summation of contractions and tetanus, ensuring adequate diastolic filling time. This contrasts sharply with skeletal muscle, which has a short refractory period and readily tetanizes. Key differential: Cardiac muscle ERP ≈ Cardiac muscle twitch duration; Skeletal muscle ERP << Skeletal muscle twitch duration.

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Autonomic Regulation of Contractile Force
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Cardiac muscle exhibits graded autonomic control of contractility (inotropy) via G-protein coupled receptors (e.g., beta-1 adrenergic receptors) activating intracellular signaling cascades (cAMP/PKA) that modulate calcium handling and cross-bridge function. Skeletal muscle force is graded solely by motor unit recruitment and firing frequency, lacking direct autonomic modulation of individual fiber contractility. (心肌表现出通过自主神经系统信号通路（如β1肾上腺素能受体）激活细胞内级联反应（cAMP/PKA）来调节收缩力（肌力），而骨骼肌的收缩力仅通过运动单位募集和发放频率来调节，缺乏对单个肌纤维收缩力的直接自主神经调节。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
An investigator is studying the mechanisms of muscle contraction. In one experiment, isolated skeletal muscle fibers are prepared. In a separate experiment, isolated ventricular trabeculae (small columns of cardiac muscle) are prepared. The investigator adds norepinephrine (NE), a beta-1 adrenergic agonist, to both preparations. In the skeletal muscle fibers, the addition of NE has no significant effect on the peak twitch force generated upon electrical stimulation. However, in the ventricular trabeculae, the addition of NE significantly increases the peak twitch force generated upon electrical stimulation. The investigator then performs a forearm ischemic exercise test on a healthy volunteer. During the exercise, the volunteer experiences significant muscle fatigue, characterized by a decrease in force production. The investigator measures serum creatine kinase (CK) levels, which are elevated post-exercise. The investigator asks: What is the fundamental difference in the mechanism by which contractile force is graded in cardiac muscle versus skeletal muscle?

(A) Skeletal muscle force is graded by altering the calcium sensitivity of troponin C, while cardiac muscle force is graded by altering the phosphorylation state of myosin light chains.
(B) Cardiac muscle force is graded by autonomic receptor signaling modulating calcium handling and cross-bridge kinetics, while skeletal muscle force is graded by motor unit recruitment and firing frequency.
(C) Skeletal muscle force is graded by altering the length-tension relationship, while cardiac muscle force is graded by altering the force-velocity relationship.
(D) Cardiac muscle force is graded by increasing the number of available cross-bridges, while skeletal muscle force is graded by increasing the speed of cross-bridge cycling.
(E) Skeletal muscle force is graded by modulating the release of calcium from the sarcoplasmic reticulum via ryanodine receptors, while cardiac muscle force is graded by modulating the influx of calcium from the extracellular space via L-type calcium channels.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Isolated Skeletal Muscle Fibers + NE:** NE is a beta-1 agonist. Skeletal muscle lacks beta-1 adrenergic receptors on the sarcolemma. Therefore, NE has no direct effect on skeletal muscle contractility. This highlights the absence of direct autonomic modulation of skeletal muscle force.
- **Isolated Ventricular Trabeculae + NE:** Cardiac muscle possesses beta-1 adrenergic receptors. NE binding activates these receptors, initiating the Gs -> cAMP -> PKA cascade. PKA phosphorylates L-type calcium channels (Cav1.2), increasing Ca2+ influx; phospholamban, accelerating SERCA2a activity (increasing SR Ca2+ reuptake); and RyR2, increasing Ca2+ release from the SR. The net effect is increased intracellular Ca2+ concentration, leading to enhanced Ca2+-troponin C binding, increased actin-myosin overlap, and thus increased peak twitch force (positive inotropy). This demonstrates graded autonomic control of cardiac contractility.
- **Forearm Ischemic Exercise + CK Elevation:** This describes typical skeletal muscle exercise physiology. Fatigue occurs due to metabolic byproducts, depletion of energy stores, etc. CK elevation indicates muscle damage/turnover, common after strenuous exercise. This context reinforces the focus on skeletal muscle physiology but is less directly relevant to the core comparison mechanism.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *fundamental difference* in how force is graded between the two muscle types.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Cardiac Muscle Force Grading:** Cardiac muscle contractility (inotropy) is finely tuned by the autonomic nervous system and circulating catecholamines. Sympathetic stimulation (via NE binding to beta-1 receptors) activates the Gs protein, leading to increased adenylyl cyclase activity and elevated intracellular cAMP levels. cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates several key proteins:
    - **L-type Calcium Channels (Cav1.2):** Phosphorylation increases the probability of opening and prolongs the open state, enhancing Ca2+ influx during the plateau phase of the action potential. This increases the Ca2+ transient.
    - **Phospholamban (PLN):** Phosphorylation relieves PLN's inhibition of SERCA2a (Sarcoplasmic/Endoplasmic Reticulum Ca2+-ATPase 2a). This accelerates Ca2+ reuptake into the SR, leading to faster relaxation (lusitropy) and potentially a larger Ca2+ load in the SR for the next beat.
    - **Ryanodine Receptors (RyR2):** Phosphorylation increases the sensitivity of RyR2 to Ca2+, enhancing Ca2+ release from the SR during the action potential plateau.
    - **Troponin I:** Phosphorylation decreases the inhibitory effect of troponin I on the actin-myosin interaction, increasing Ca2+ sensitivity.
    - **Myofilament Proteins (e.g., Myosin Binding Protein C):** Phosphorylation can alter the passive stiffness and Ca2+ sensitivity of the myofilaments.
    The net result of these PKA-mediated phosphorylations is increased intracellular Ca2+ concentration and enhanced Ca2+ sensitivity of the contractile apparatus, leading to a stronger contraction (increased peak force) and faster relaxation. This is a graded response dependent on autonomic input.
- **Skeletal Muscle Force Grading:** Skeletal muscle force is graded primarily by two mechanisms controlled by the somatic nervous system:
    - **Motor Unit Recruitment:** The nervous system recruits increasing numbers of motor units (a motor neuron and all the muscle fibers it innervates) as force demand increases. Each motor unit contributes a fixed amount of force (twitch force). More motor units = more force.
    - **Rate Coding (Frequency Coding):** The frequency of action potentials sent by a motor neuron increases. Higher frequency stimulation leads to summation of twitches (incomplete tetanus) and eventually a sustained contraction (complete tetanus) with greater force than a single twitch.
    Skeletal muscle lacks direct autonomic innervation that modulates the contractility of individual fibers via G-protein signaling cascades affecting Ca2+ handling or cross-bridge kinetics in the way cardiac muscle does. Acetylcholine released at the neuromuscular junction triggers muscle fiber depolarization and excitation-contraction coupling, but the *strength* of this coupling per fiber is not directly modulated by circulating hormones or autonomic nerves in the same manner as cardiac muscle.

##### 3. Synthesis: Comparison
Cardiac muscle force is graded *per beat* by modulating the Ca2+ transient and Ca2+ sensitivity through autonomic signaling (primarily beta-1 adrenergic stimulation via cAMP/PKA). Skeletal muscle force is graded *by recruiting more fibers* and *increasing the firing rate* of motor neurons, without direct autonomic modulation of individual fiber contractility strength.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While cardiac muscle force is graded partly by altering the phosphorylation state of troponin I (increasing Ca2+ sensitivity), skeletal muscle force is *not* graded by altering troponin C sensitivity. Troponin C binds Ca2+, and its sensitivity is not the primary mechanism for grading skeletal muscle force. Also, skeletal muscle force is not graded by altering myosin light chain phosphorylation in the context of autonomic regulation (though phosphorylation does occur, it's not the *mechanism* of force grading).
- (C): Incorrect. While the length-tension relationship is fundamental to *all* muscle contraction (both cardiac and skeletal), it's not the mechanism by which *graded* force is achieved in response to physiological stimuli. The length-tension relationship describes the force generated at a given length, not how force is increased or decreased. Similarly, the force-velocity relationship describes how force changes with contraction speed, not the mechanism of force grading.
- (D): Incorrect. Cardiac muscle *does* increase the number of active cross-bridges (via increased Ca2+) when stimulated, and this contributes to increased force. Skeletal muscle also increases active cross-bridges via recruitment and rate coding. However, this option doesn't capture the *fundamental difference* in the *regulatory mechanism* (autonomic vs. somatic). Furthermore, increasing the *speed* of cross-bridge cycling is more related to relaxation (lusitropy) in cardiac muscle and is not the primary way force is graded in either muscle type.
- (E): Incorrect. Both cardiac and skeletal muscle rely on calcium release from the SR (RyR1 in skeletal, RyR2 in cardiac) and calcium influx from the extracellular space (via L-type Ca2+ channels, Cav1.2, primarily in cardiac muscle, and DHPRs coupled to RyR1 in skeletal muscle). However, this option misattributes the primary mechanism of force grading. While modulating Ca2+ release and influx is crucial for cardiac inotropy, skeletal muscle force grading relies on motor unit recruitment and firing frequency, not direct modulation of these Ca2+ mechanisms by autonomic signals.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cardiac muscle contractility is graded by autonomic nervous system input (primarily sympathetic beta-1 stimulation) acting via G-protein coupled receptors and intracellular signaling cascades (cAMP/PKA) that modulate Ca2+ handling and cross-bridge function. Skeletal muscle force is graded by somatic motor neuron activity through motor unit recruitment and firing frequency. This fundamental difference reflects the distinct physiological roles of these muscle types (cardiac: continuous, graded output; skeletal: voluntary, powerful, fatigable contractions). Key differentials include smooth muscle (graded autonomic control via MLCK/MLCP phosphorylation) and non-muscle smooth muscle (e.g., vascular, visceral).

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Cardiac Muscle Relaxation & SERCA2a Regulation
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Phospholamban (PLN) is an inhibitory protein of SERCA2a in cardiac muscle. Phosphorylation of PLN by PKA relieves this inhibition, enhancing SERCA2a activity, accelerating calcium reuptake into the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR), and promoting faster cardiac muscle relaxation (positive lusitropy). (心肌中磷酸化酶 (PLN) 是 SERCA2a 的抑制蛋白。PKA 磷酸化 PLN 能够解除这种抑制作用，增强 SERCA2a 活性，加速钙离子重新摄入肌浆网 (SR)，并促进更快的肌张力松弛（正向肌张力变化）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient is undergoing an echocardiogram to assess cardiac function during exercise stress testing. Baseline echocardiography shows normal left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) and wall motion. During the test, the patient's heart rate increases from 70 bpm to 140 bpm, and blood pressure rises from 120/80 mmHg to 160/100 mmHg. Real-time echocardiography during peak exercise reveals a significant decrease in the isovolumetric relaxation time (IVRT) compared to baseline measurements. The patient is then administered intravenous dobutamine, a beta-1 adrenergic agonist. Post-dobutamine echocardiography shows a further reduction in IVRT, indicating accelerated ventricular relaxation. The patient has no known history of cardiac disease or genetic predisposition to cardiomyopathies.

Which of the following molecular events BEST explains the accelerated ventricular relaxation observed after dobutamine administration?

(A) Increased L-type calcium channel (Cav1.2) open probability, leading to enhanced calcium influx during systole and increased SR calcium load.
(B) Phosphorylation of phospholamban (PLN) by protein kinase A (PKA), resulting in disinhibition of SERCA2a and enhanced calcium reuptake into the sarcoplasmic reticulum.
(C) Increased activity of the sodium-calcium exchanger (NCX), leading to enhanced calcium extrusion from the cytosol into the extracellular space.
(D) Decreased sensitivity of troponin C to calcium, requiring higher cytosolic calcium concentrations for cross-bridge detachment and promoting faster relaxation.
(E) Increased activity of ryanodine receptors (RyR2) on the sarcoplasmic reticulum, leading to enhanced calcium release during diastole and increased SR calcium load.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** A healthy 45-year-old male undergoing exercise stress testing with echocardiography. This sets the context for assessing cardiac function under physiological stress (increased sympathetic tone).
- **Baseline Findings:** Normal LVEF and wall motion indicate healthy baseline cardiac contractility and structure.
- **Exercise Response:** Increased heart rate and blood pressure during exercise are expected physiological responses, indicating increased sympathetic nervous system activity. The echocardiogram shows a decreased IVRT during exercise, suggesting faster relaxation is already occurring due to sympathetic stimulation.
- **Dobutamine Administration:** Dobutamine is a beta-1 adrenergic agonist, mimicking sympathetic stimulation.
- **Post-Dobutamine Findings:** A further reduction in IVRT after dobutamine administration confirms that beta-1 adrenergic stimulation accelerates ventricular relaxation.
- **Question Stem:** The question asks for the molecular mechanism underlying this accelerated relaxation (positive lusitropy) induced by beta-1 adrenergic stimulation (via dobutamine).

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Beta-Adrenergic Stimulation:** Beta-1 adrenergic receptor activation by catecholamines (like epinephrine released during exercise or administered as dobutamine) leads to activation of adenylyl cyclase via Gs protein.
- **cAMP and PKA Activation:** Adenylyl cyclase converts ATP to cyclic AMP (cAMP). cAMP acts as a second messenger, activating protein kinase A (PKA).
- **Phospholamban (PLN) Phosphorylation:** PKA phosphorylates several target proteins in cardiac myocytes, including phospholamban (PLN), which is located on the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) membrane.
- **PLN Function:** Unphosphorylated PLN acts as a tonic inhibitor of the sarcoplasmic/endoplasmic reticulum Ca2+-ATPase 2a (SERCA2a). It binds to SERCA2a, reducing its affinity for Ca2+ and slowing down the rate of Ca2+ reuptake into the SR.
- **Effect of PKA Phosphorylation on PLN:** PKA phosphorylates PLN at specific serine residues (primarily Ser-16). This phosphorylation induces a conformational change in PLN, causing it to dissociate from SERCA2a.
- **SERCA2a Activation:** The dissociation of phosphorylated PLN from SERCA2a removes the inhibition. SERCA2a is now free to function at its maximal rate.
- **Accelerated Calcium Reuptake:** Activated SERCA2a rapidly pumps Ca2+ from the cytosol back into the SR lumen. This rapid removal of cytosolic Ca2+ leads to a faster decrease in intracellular Ca2+ concentration.
- **Faster Relaxation (Positive Lusitropy):** The rapid decrease in cytosolic Ca2+ concentration causes troponin C to release Ca2+, allowing tropomyosin to block myosin-binding sites on actin. This leads to faster detachment of cross-bridges and accelerated muscle relaxation. This phenomenon is known as positive lusitropy.
- **Therefore:** PKA phosphorylation of PLN leads to disinhibition of SERCA2a, enhanced calcium reuptake, and accelerated cardiac muscle relaxation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased L-type calcium channel (Cav1.2) open probability is primarily stimulated by depolarization during the action potential and is enhanced by beta-adrenergic stimulation via PKA phosphorylation. While this increases calcium influx during systole (leading to increased contractility - positive inotropy), it does *not* directly explain the accelerated *relaxation* (lusitropy). Increased SR calcium load might slightly enhance subsequent contraction, but the primary mechanism for faster relaxation is enhanced calcium removal.
- (C): The sodium-calcium exchanger (NCX) extrudes calcium from the cell in exchange for sodium influx. While NCX contributes to calcium removal, its role is less significant than SERCA2a in cardiac muscle relaxation, especially under conditions of high cytosolic calcium. Furthermore, beta-adrenergic stimulation does not primarily act by enhancing NCX activity; the main effect on relaxation is via SERCA2a regulation.
- (D): Decreased sensitivity of troponin C to calcium would require *more* calcium to be removed for relaxation to occur, thus *slowing* relaxation, not accelerating it. Beta-adrenergic stimulation does not decrease troponin C sensitivity; rather, it enhances the *rate* of calcium removal, allowing relaxation to occur faster at a given calcium concentration.
- (E): Ryanodine receptors (RyR2) are calcium release channels on the SR. Increased RyR2 activity would lead to *increased* calcium release into the cytosol during systole (enhancing contractility) or potentially during diastole (leading to diastolic dysfunction or arrhythmias), not faster relaxation. Beta-adrenergic stimulation can slightly increase RyR2 phosphorylation (by PKA or CaMKII), but this effect is primarily related to contractility, not relaxation. The key mechanism for accelerated relaxation is enhanced calcium *reuptake*, not release.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is the role of phospholamban (PLN) as a regulator of SERCA2a activity in cardiac muscle. Beta-adrenergic stimulation leads to PKA phosphorylation of PLN, which disinhibits SERCA2a, accelerating calcium reuptake and promoting faster relaxation (positive lusitropy). This contrasts with skeletal muscle, where SERCA1 is not regulated by PLN and SERCA2a is absent. Also, remember that PLN is primarily found in cardiac muscle and slow-twitch skeletal muscle, not fast-twitch skeletal muscle. This mechanism is crucial for understanding the effects of sympathetic stimulation on cardiac function.

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Cardiac vs. Skeletal Muscle Length-Tension Relationship
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Comparison of the length-tension relationship in cardiac muscle (ascending limb, Frank-Starling mechanism) versus skeletal muscle (operating near optimal length, descending limb upon stretch).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A cardiovascular physiologist is comparing the contractile properties of resting skeletal muscle and resting left ventricular myocardium. She plots the operating points of both tissues on their respective length-tension curves. For resting skeletal muscle, the sarcomere length is measured at 2.1 µm, corresponding to the peak of the length-tension curve. For resting left ventricular myocardium at end-diastole, the sarcomere length is measured at 1.85 µm, which is on the steep ascending limb of the cardiac length-tension curve. The physiologist then performs an experiment where she increases preload in the isolated heart, causing the ventricular sarcomeres to stretch to 1.95 µm. Simultaneously, she stretches the isolated resting skeletal muscle preparation to 2.3 µm. She observes that the ventricular muscle generates significantly more force after stretching, while the skeletal muscle generates less force after stretching. Which of the following best explains this difference in response to stretch?

(A) Skeletal muscle contains predominantly fast-twitch fibers, which are more sensitive to changes in sarcomere length than cardiac muscle fibers.
(B) Cardiac muscle operates at a sarcomere length below its optimal length (L0), so stretching increases cross-bridge overlap and calcium sensitivity, whereas skeletal muscle operates near its optimal length, so stretching decreases cross-bridge overlap.
(C) Skeletal muscle relies primarily on aerobic metabolism, which is less efficient at generating ATP for contraction compared to the anaerobic metabolism favored by cardiac muscle.
(D) The troponin-tropomyosin complex in skeletal muscle is more sensitive to calcium than in cardiac muscle, leading to a greater reduction in force upon stretch.
(E) Cardiac muscle has a higher density of T-tubules and sarcoplasmic reticulum compared to skeletal muscle, allowing for more efficient calcium release and uptake during stretch.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Setting:** Cardiovascular physiology lab comparing skeletal and cardiac muscle.
- **Key Measurement 1:** Resting skeletal muscle sarcomere length = 2.1 µm. This is stated to be at the peak of its length-tension curve (optimal length, L0).
- **Key Measurement 2:** Resting ventricular myocardium sarcomere length = 1.85 µm. This is stated to be on the steep ascending limb of its length-tension curve, below L0.
- **Experimental Manipulation:** Both tissues are stretched. Ventricular sarcomere length increases to 1.95 µm. Skeletal muscle sarcomere length increases to 2.3 µm.
- **Observation:** Ventricular muscle force increases after stretch. Skeletal muscle force decreases after stretch.
- **Question:** Why does stretching cardiac muscle increase force, while stretching skeletal muscle decreases force?

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Length-Tension Relationship:** The force generated by a muscle fiber is dependent on its initial sarcomere length. This relationship is described by the length-tension curve.
- **Skeletal Muscle:** At rest, skeletal muscle sarcomeres are typically near their optimal length (L0 ≈ 2.0-2.2 µm), where the overlap between actin and myosin filaments is maximal, allowing for the greatest number of cross-bridges to form. Stretching skeletal muscle beyond L0 moves it onto the descending limb of the length-tension curve. This reduces the overlap between actin and myosin filaments (especially the overlap between thick and thin filaments at the Z-disc ends), decreasing the number of potential cross-bridges and thus reducing the force generated upon activation.
- **Cardiac Muscle:** At rest (end-diastole), cardiac muscle sarcomeres are shorter than their optimal length (L0 ≈ 2.0-2.2 µm), typically around 1.8-1.9 µm. This means they are operating on the steep ascending limb of the length-tension curve. Increasing preload stretches the ventricular walls, increasing the end-diastolic volume and stretching the sarcomeres towards L0. This increased stretch enhances the overlap between actin and myosin filaments, increasing the number of potential cross-bridges. Furthermore, stretching cardiac muscle increases the sensitivity of the contractile proteins (troponin C) to calcium, meaning that for a given intracellular calcium concentration, more cross-bridges will form. Both increased overlap and increased calcium sensitivity contribute to the increased force generated (Frank-Starling mechanism).
- **Comparison:** The key difference lies in the *initial* operating point on the length-tension curve. Skeletal muscle rests near the peak, so stretching *reduces* force. Cardiac muscle rests on the ascending limb, so stretching *increases* force towards the peak.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): While skeletal muscle contains different fiber types (fast-twitch vs. slow-twitch), the fundamental difference in response to stretch between skeletal and cardiac muscle is not primarily due to fiber type composition. Both types of muscle exhibit a decrease in force when stretched beyond their optimal length. The core issue is the *starting point* on the length-tension curve.
- (C): Metabolic differences (aerobic vs. anaerobic) are relevant to muscle fatigue and endurance but do not explain the immediate mechanical response to stretch as described by the length-tension relationship. Both skeletal and cardiac muscle utilize aerobic metabolism extensively.
- (D): The sensitivity of the troponin-tropomyosin complex to calcium is generally similar between skeletal and cardiac muscle, although cardiac muscle does show increased calcium sensitivity with stretch. However, the primary reason for the difference in response to stretch is the initial sarcomere length relative to L0, which affects cross-bridge overlap. Stating that skeletal muscle's complex is *more* sensitive is incorrect and doesn't explain the observed phenomenon.
- (E): Differences in T-tubule and sarcoplasmic reticulum structure exist, but they primarily affect the speed and efficiency of calcium handling (excitation-contraction coupling), not the fundamental length-tension relationship or the response to passive stretch. While calcium handling is crucial for contraction, the length-tension relationship describes the force generated at a given length *when activated*, and the difference here stems from the passive mechanical properties related to filament overlap at rest.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The Frank-Starling mechanism in the heart relies on the fact that cardiac muscle operates on the ascending limb of its length-tension curve at rest. Increased preload stretches the muscle towards its optimal length, increasing force. Skeletal muscle, resting near its optimal length, operates on the descending limb, so stretch decreases force. This difference is crucial for understanding cardiac function and distinguishing it from skeletal muscle mechanics. Key differentials include understanding the position of the resting sarcomere length relative to L0 for each muscle type and the shape of their respective length-tension curves.

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Smooth Muscle Ultrastructure & Regulation
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Smooth muscle lacks sarcomeres and the troponin complex, relying instead on calmodulin and myosin light chain kinase (MLCK) for calcium-dependent contraction. (平滑肌缺乏肌节和肌动蛋白络合物，依赖钙调蛋白和肌球蛋白轻链激酶 (MLCK) 进行钙依赖性收缩。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male patient presents with chronic abdominal pain and intermittent nausea. An upper endoscopy reveals diffuse thickening of the stomach wall. Biopsies are taken from the gastric muscularis externa and the jejunal muscularis propria. Histopathological examination of the gastric biopsy under polarized light microscopy shows spindle-shaped cells arranged in sheets, lacking the characteristic periodic banding pattern seen in skeletal or cardiac muscle. Electron microscopy confirms the absence of Z-discs, A-bands, and I-bands. The jejunal biopsy exhibits similar findings. Immunostaining for specific contractile proteins is performed. Staining for actin and myosin is positive in both biopsies, but staining for troponin T is completely negative in both smooth muscle preparations. The pathologist notes the presence of dense bodies within the cytoplasm of the smooth muscle cells, serving as anchoring points for the actin filaments. The patient's symptoms are eventually attributed to gastroparesis secondary to underlying systemic sclerosis, which can affect smooth muscle function.

Which regulatory protein complex, essential for striated muscle contraction, is completely absent in smooth muscle?

(A) Titin
(B) Nebulin
(C) Troponin complex (Troponin C, I, and T)
(D) Myomesin
(E) Alpha-actinin

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with abdominal pain/nausea, endoscopic findings of gastric wall thickening. This points towards a potential gastrointestinal motility disorder or structural abnormality.
- **Biopsy Location:** Gastric muscularis externa and jejunal muscularis propria. These are layers containing smooth muscle.
- **Polarized Light Microscopy:** Absence of periodic banding (light/dark bands) in both biopsies. This is a key feature distinguishing smooth muscle from striated muscle (skeletal and cardiac), which possess sarcomeres.
- **Electron Microscopy:** Confirms absence of Z-discs, A-bands, and I-bands. These are the ultrastructural components defining the sarcomere in striated muscle.
- **Immunostaining:** Positive for actin and myosin (contractile proteins present in both smooth and striated muscle) but negative for troponin T. This is the crucial finding. Troponin T is a specific component of the troponin complex found in striated muscle.
- **Dense Bodies:** Mentioned as anchoring points for actin filaments in smooth muscle. This is characteristic of smooth muscle ultrastructure.
- **Underlying Condition:** Systemic sclerosis (scleroderma) can affect smooth muscle, causing fibrosis and dysfunction, potentially explaining the patient's symptoms.
- **Question Stem:** Asks specifically which regulatory protein complex, essential for *striated* muscle contraction, is absent in smooth muscle.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Striated Muscle Contraction Regulation:** In skeletal and cardiac muscle, contraction is initiated by an increase in intracellular calcium ([Ca2+]i). Calcium binds to Troponin C (TnC), a subunit of the troponin complex (TnC, TnI, TnT). This binding causes a conformational change in the troponin complex, which moves tropomyosin away from the myosin-binding sites on the actin filament. This allows the myosin heads to bind to actin, initiating the cross-bridge cycle and muscle contraction.
- **Smooth Muscle Contraction Regulation:** Smooth muscle contraction is also calcium-dependent, but the regulatory mechanism is different. Increased [Ca2+]i leads to calcium binding to calmodulin (CaM). The Ca2+-CaM complex then activates myosin light chain kinase (MLCK). MLCK phosphorylates the regulatory light chain on the myosin II heads. Phosphorylation allows the myosin heads to bind to actin and initiate the cross-bridge cycle. Unlike striated muscle, smooth muscle lacks the troponin complex. The actin and myosin filaments are arranged obliquely, not in sarcomeres, and are anchored to dense bodies. Contraction causes the cell to shorten and twist.
- **Key Difference:** The absence of the troponin complex (TnC, TnI, TnT) is the defining difference in the calcium-regulatory mechanism between striated and smooth muscle. The vignette explicitly states the absence of troponin T staining in the smooth muscle biopsies, confirming this fundamental difference.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Titin: Titin is a giant elastic protein found in striated muscle sarcomeres. It spans from the Z-disc to the M-line, providing structural support and contributing to passive elasticity. It is absent in smooth muscle, but it is not the *regulatory* protein complex mentioned in the question stem.
- (B) Nebulin: Nebulin is another large protein found in skeletal muscle sarcomeres. It is associated with the thin filaments (actin) and is thought to regulate thin filament length. It is absent in smooth muscle and cardiac muscle, but again, it is not the primary *regulatory* complex for calcium-dependent contraction.
- (C) Troponin complex (Troponin C, I, and T): This complex is the direct calcium sensor and regulator of the thin filament in striated muscle. Calcium binds to TnC, initiating the conformational changes that expose myosin-binding sites on actin. As detailed above and confirmed by the vignette's negative immunostaining, this complex is completely absent in smooth muscle. This is the correct answer.
- (D) Myomesin: Myomesin is a protein found in the M-line of striated muscle sarcomeres, linking thick filaments together. It is absent in smooth muscle, but it is not involved in the direct regulation of contraction by calcium.
- (E) Alpha-actinin: Alpha-actinin is a protein that cross-links actin filaments. It is found in both striated and smooth muscle, although its specific isoforms and roles may differ. It is involved in maintaining the structural integrity of the actin cytoskeleton, particularly at Z-discs in striated muscle and dense bodies in smooth muscle. It is not the regulatory complex absent in smooth muscle.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Smooth muscle lacks sarcomeres and the troponin complex, relying on calmodulin and MLCK for calcium-dependent contraction. This contrasts sharply with striated muscle (skeletal and cardiac), which uses the troponin-tropomyosin system to regulate actin-myosin interaction in response to calcium. Key structural differences include the absence of Z-discs, A-bands, I-bands, and dense bodies (instead of Z-discs) in smooth muscle.
